        Max ratio $3/2$.
        
        So $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    
    Let's check if $q$ could be derived from partial blocks?
    We assumed that the sum of a full block of 3 terms in GP is related to the sum of the next full block by $q^3$.
    Is it possible to use partial blocks?
    Suppose GP covers parts of Block 1 and Block 2, but not full blocks?
    We proved that a length 9 interval always contains at least 2 full blocks.
    So we can always find 2 full blocks inside the GP.
    Let the full blocks be Block $j$ and Block $j+1$.
    The terms of Block $j$ are $a_{3j-2}, a_{3j-1}, a_{3j}$.
    These are 3 consecutive terms in the GP.
    Let's say they are at positions $p, p+1, p+2$ in the GP.
    Then terms of Block $j+1$ are $a_{3j+1}, a_{3j+2}, a_{3j+3}$.
    These are at positions $p+3, p+4, p+5$ in the GP.
    The sum of terms at $p, p+1, p+2$ is $S_1$.
    The sum of terms at $p+3, p+4, p+5$ is $S_2$.
    Since it's a GP, $S_2 = q^3 S_1$.
    We know $S_1 = 2j$ and $S_2 = 2(j+1)$.
    So $q^3 = \frac{2(j+1)}{2j} = \frac{j+1}{j}$.
    This derivation holds regardless of where the GP starts, as long as it contains two full consecutive blocks.
    And we proved it always contains at least 2.
    And if it contains 3, we have a contradiction.
    So it must contain exactly 2.
    This forces $q^3 = \frac{j+1}{j}$ for some integer $j$.
    Also constraints on $j$ derived from existence of such GP.
    We found $j \ge 2$.
    Max $q$ at $j=2$, $q^3 = 1.5$.
    
    Is it possible that the "3 full blocks" case is not a contradiction?
    Let's re-verify.
    Blocks $j, j+1, j+2$.
    Sums $2j, 2(j+1), 2(j+2)$.
    Ratios $r_1 = \frac{2(j+1)}{2j} = \frac{j+1}{j}$.
    $r_2 = \frac{2(j+2)}{2(j+1)} = \frac{j+2}{j+1}$.
    In a GP, the ratio of sum of 3 consecutive terms to the sum of the next 3 consecutive terms is always $q^3$.
    So $r_1$ must equal $r_2$ must equal $q^3$.
    $\frac{j+1}{j} = \frac{j+2}{j+1}$.
    $(j+1)^2 = j(j+2) \implies j^2+2j+1 = j^2+2j \implies 1=0$.
    Contradiction.
    So yes, 3 full blocks is impossible.
    
    Wait, what if the 3 blocks are not consecutive in the GP?
    But indices are consecutive.
    Block $j$ is indices $3j-2, 3j-1, 3j$.
    Block $j+1$ is indices $3j+1, 3j+2, 3j+3$.
    They are adjacent.
    So terms are adjacent in GP.
    So yes, ratios must match.
    
    So the logic holds.
    Max $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    
    Let's check if $q$ can be 1?
    If $q=1$, $a_n$ constant?
    If $a_n = c$, then $3n c = n^2+n$.
    $c = \frac{n+1}{3}$. Depends on $n$. Impossible.
    So $q \ne 1$.
    
    Let's check if $q$ can be very large?
    If $q$ large, terms grow fast.
    Sums of blocks grow.
    But sums are fixed $2n$.
    So $q$ cannot be too large.
    Specifically, if we have a block sum $S$, next block sum is $q^3 S$.
    If $q > 1$, sums grow.
    We need $S_{next} > S_{current}$.
    $2(n+1) > 2n$. True.
    But growth rate must match.
    $\frac{S_{next}}{S_{current}} = q^3$.
    $\frac{2n+2}{2n} = 1 + 1/n$.
    As $n \to \infty$, ratio $\to 1$, so $q \to 1$.
    For small $n$, ratio is larger.
    Max ratio is at smallest $n$ allowed.
    Smallest allowed $n$ for 2 blocks is 2.
    Ratio 1.5.
    If we could use $n=1$ (blocks 1 and 2), ratio 2.
    But $n=1$ requires GP to cover blocks 1 and 2 without 3.
    Block 1: 1,2,3. Block 2: 4,5,6.
    Union 1..6.
    Interval length 9 containing 1..6 must be $[k, k+8]$.
    $k \le 1$ and $k+8 \ge 6$.
    $k \le 1$.
    If $k=1$, interval $[1, 9]$. Contains 7,8,9 (Block 3).
    So we always pick up Block 3.
    So $n=1$ impossible.
    
    What if the GP terms are not aligned with blocks?
    Wait, I assumed that the sum of a full block (which is a subset of GP terms) scales by $q^3$.
    This is true regardless of alignment?
    Let GP terms be $t_1, t_2, t_3, t_4, t_5, t_6, \dots$.
    Block 1 sum is sum of some 3 consecutive terms, say $t_a + t_{a+1} + t_{a+2}$.
    Block 2 sum is sum of next 3 consecutive terms $t_{a+3} + t_{a+4} + t_{a+5}$.
    Wait, Block 1 and Block 2 are adjacent in indices.
    Block 1 indices: $I_1, I_2, I_3$.
    Block 2 indices: $I_1+1, I_2+1, I_3+1$.
    So yes, they correspond to consecutive triples in the GP sequence.
    So the sum ratio property holds.
    
    Is it possible that a block is not fully contained in the GP?
    Yes, the partial blocks at the ends.
    But we established that there must be at least 2 fully contained blocks.
    Let the fully contained blocks be $B_x$ and $B_y$.
    Since they are fully contained in a contiguous interval of indices, and blocks are contiguous partitions of indices, $B_x$ and $B_y$ must be adjacent blocks?
    Suppose there is a gap.
    Indices in GP: $[k, k+8]$.
    Suppose Block $p$ is fully inside. Indices $\{3p-2, 3p-1, 3p\} \subseteq [k, k+8]$.
    Suppose Block $q$ ($q > p$) is fully inside.
    Indices $\{3q-2, 3q-1, 3q\} \subseteq [k, k+8]$.
    If $q > p+1$, then Block $p+1$ (indices $3p+1, \dots$) must be "skipped"?
    But indices are integers.
    If $\{3p-2, \dots, 3p\} \subseteq [k, k+8]$ and $\{3q-2, \dots, 3q\} \subseteq [k, k+8]$.
    Since $q \ge p+2$, indices of Block $p$ are $\le 3p$.
    Indices of Block $q$ are $\ge 3p+4$.
    So there is a gap between $3p$ and $3p+4$?
    Indices $3p+1, 3p+2, 3p+3$ are in between.
    These form Block $p+1$.
    Since $[k, k+8]$ is an interval of integers, it cannot skip integers.
    If it contains $3p$ and $3p+4$, it must contain $3p+1, 3p+2, 3p+3$.
    So Block $p+1$ must be fully contained as well.
    So fully contained blocks must form a consecutive sequence.
    So if there are at least 2, there must be a pair of consecutive fully contained blocks.
    Let them be Block $j$ and Block $j+1$.
    Then their sums ratio is $q^3$.
    So $q^3 = \frac{2(j+1)}{2j}$.
    And we need to ensure that we can have exactly these 2 blocks (or at least these 2, but not 3).
    Actually, if we have 3, contradiction.
    So we need exactly 2.
    We found that for $j=1$, we are forced to have 3.
    For $j=2$, we can have exactly 2.
    So $j=2$ is the optimal case.
    $q^3 = 3/2$.
    
    Let's check $j=2$ case explicitly.
    Blocks 2 and 3.
    Block 2: indices 4,5,6. Sum 4.
    Block 3: indices 7,8,9. Sum 6.
    GP must contain 4,5,6 and 7,8,9.
    So GP interval must cover $[4, 9]$.
    Length 9 interval covering $[4, 9]$.
    Interval $[k, k+8]$.
    $k \le 4$ and $k+8 \ge 9 \implies k \ge 1$.
    Possible $k \in \{1, 2, 3, 4\}$.
    
    If $k=1$: Interval $[1, 9]$.
    Contains 1,2,3 (Block 1).
    So contains Blocks 1, 2, 3.
    3 blocks. Contradiction.
    
    If $k=2$: Interval $[2, 10]$.
    Contains 4,5,6 (Block 2).
    Contains 7,8,9 (Block 3).
    Does it contain Block 1 (1,2,3)?
    Contains 2,3. Missing 1. No.
    Does it contain Block 4 (10,11,12)?
    Contains 10. Missing 11,12. No.
    So exactly Blocks 2 and 3 are fully contained.
    Valid.
    GP terms $a_2, \dots, a_{10}$.
    $a_2, a_3$ are in Block 1 (partial).
    $a_4, a_5, a_6$ are Block 2 (full).
    $a_7, a_8, a_9$ are Block 3 (full).
    $a_{10}$ is in Block 4 (partial).
    Sums of full blocks are 4 and 6.
    Terms in Block 2 correspond to some triple in GP.
    Terms in Block 3 correspond to next triple.
    Ratio 1.5.
    $q^3 = 1.5$.
    This works.
    
    If $k=3$: Interval $[3, 11]$.
    Contains 4,5,6.
    Contains 7,8,9.
    Contains 10,11. No 12.
    Contains 3. No 1,2.
    So exactly Blocks 2 and 3.
    Valid.
    
    If $k=4$: Interval $[4, 12]$.
    Contains 4,5,6.
    Contains 7,8,9.
    Contains 10,11,12 (Block 4).
    So Blocks 2, 3, 4.
    3 blocks. Contradiction.
    
    So $k=2$ and $k=3$ are valid starting positions for $j=2$.
    $k=2$ corresponds to $3m+2$ with $m=0$.
    $k=3$ corresponds to $3m$ with $m=1$.
    Both valid.
    
    So $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$ is achievable.
    Can we go higher?
    Need $q^3 > 1.5$.
    Need ratio of consecutive block sums $> 1.5$.
    Ratio is $1 + 1/n$.
    Need $1 + 1/n > 1.5 \implies 1/n > 0.5 \implies n < 2$.
    So $n=1$.
    Blocks 1 and 2.
    Ratio 2.
    But we saw that covering Blocks 1 and 2 forces covering Block 3 (if length 9).
    Wait, let's re-verify this strictly.
    Block 1: $\{1, 2, 3\}$.
    Block 2: $\{4, 5, 6\}$.
    GP interval $I = [k, k+8]$.
    We need $\{1, 2, 3\} \subseteq I$ and $\{4, 5, 6\} \subseteq I$.
    Union is $\{1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6\}$.
    So $[1, 6] \subseteq [k, k+8]$.
    $k \le 1$ and $k+8 \ge 6$.
    $k \le 1$.
    Since $k \ge 1$, $k=1$.
    Interval is $[1, 9]$.
    Indices $\{1, \dots, 9\}$.
    Check Block 3: $\{7, 8, 9\}$.
    Is $\{7, 8, 9\} \subseteq \{1, \dots, 9\}$? Yes.
    So Block 3 is fully contained.
    So we have 3 blocks.
    Contradiction.
    
    So $n=1$ is impossible.
    Thus max ratio is for $n=2$, which is 1.5.
    
    Result $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$.
    Value $\approx 1.1447$.

    Let's check if $q$ can be negative?
    If $q < 0$, $q^3 < 0$.
    But ratios of sums of blocks are positive.
    Wait, sums of blocks are $2n > 0$.
    However, sum of 3 terms in GP with negative ratio?
    If $q < 0$, terms alternate sign.
    Sum of 3 terms $x + xq + xq^2$.
    Can be negative?
    If $x > 0, q = -2$. $1 - 2 + 4 = 3 > 0$.
    If $x > 0, q = -0.5$. $1 - 0.5 + 0.25 = 0.75 > 0$.
    Actually $1+q+q^2 > 0$ for all real $q$.
    So sum of 3 consecutive terms has same sign as first term.
    If we have two full blocks in GP, say Block $j$ and $j+1$.
    Sum $S_j = 2j > 0$.
    Sum $S_{j+1} = 2(j+1) > 0$.
    Both sums are positive.
    So the first term of the triple in Block $j$ must be positive.
    The first term of the triple in Block $j+1$ must be positive.
    The first term of Block $j+1$ triple is $q^3$ times the first term of Block $j$ triple.
    So $q^3 = S_{j+1}/S_j > 0$.
    So $q$ must be positive.
    So negative $q$ is ruled out.
    
    What if the triples are not aligned with the "first term" logic?
    Let terms be $t_1, t_2, t_3$. Sum $S_1$.
    Next terms $t_4, t_5, t_6$. Sum $S_2$.
    $t_4 = t_1 q^3$.
    $t_5 = t_2 q^3$.
    $t_6 = t_3 q^3$.
    $S_2 = q^3 S_1$.
    This relation holds regardless of signs.
    Since $S_1, S_2 > 0$, $q^3 > 0$.
    So $q > 0$.
    
    So $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5}$ is the global maximum.

    Let's do a quick sanity check on the calculation $S_{3n} = n^2+n$.
    $n=1, S_3 = 2$.
    $n=2, S_6 = 6$.
    $a_4+a_5+a_6 = 4$.
    $n=3, S_9 = 12$.
    $a_7+a_8+a_9 = 6$.
    GP terms $a_2, \dots, a_{10}$.
    $a_4, a_5, a_6$ are terms 3,4,5 of GP?
    $a_2 = g_1$ (using 1-based indexing for GP terms).
    $a_3 = g_2$.
    $a_4 = g_3$.
    $a_5 = g_4$.
    $a_6 = g_5$.
    Block 2 sum: $g_3 + g_4 + g_5 = 4$.
    Block 3 sum: $g_6 + g_7 + g_8 = 6$.
    $g_6 = g_3 q^3$.
    $g_7 = g_4 q^3$.
    $g_8 = g_5 q^3$.
    Sum2 = $q^3 (g_3 + g_4 + g_5) = 4 q^3$.
    $4 q^3 = 6 \implies q^3 = 1.5$.
    Consistent.
    Check Block 1 sum constraint?
    $a_1 + a_2 + a_3 = 2$.
    $a_2 = g_1$. $a_3 = g_2 = g_1 q$.
    $a_1 + g_1(1+q) = 2$.
    We need to find $g_1$ (which is $A$).
    From Block 2 sum: $g_3(1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    $g_3 = g_1 q^2$.
    So $g_1 q^2 (1+q+q^2) = 4$.
    $g_1 = \frac{4}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
    Then $a_1 = 2 - \frac{4(1+q)}{q^2(1+q+q^2)}$.
    For $q = \sqrt[3]{1.5} \approx 1.145$.
    $q^2 \approx 1.31$.
    $1+q \approx 2.145$.
    $1+q+q^2 \approx 3.455$.
    $g_1 \approx \frac{4}{1.31 \times 3.455} \approx \frac{4}{4.526} \approx 0.88$.
    $a_1 \approx 2 - 0.88(2.145)/4.526 \times 4$?
    Wait, term is $\frac{4(1+q)}{q^2(1+q+q^2)} = \frac{4}{q^2(1+q+q^2)} (1+q) = g_1(1+q)$.
    $g_1(1+q) \approx 0.88 \times 2.145 \approx 1.89$.
    $a_1 \approx 2 - 1.89 = 0.11$.
    Positive. Real.
    Check Block 4 sum ($a_{10} + a_{11} + a_{12} = 8$).
    $a_{10} = g_9 = g_1 q^8$.
    $a_{10} \approx 0.88 \times (1.145)^8 \approx 0.88 \times 2.98 \approx 2.62$.
    $a_{11}, a_{12}$ can be chosen.
    So valid.
    
    What about $k=3$?
    GP $a_3, \dots, a_{11}$.
    Block 2 sum ($a_4, a_5, a_6$) is 4.
    Terms $a_4, a_5, a_6$ correspond to $g_2, g_3, g_4$ in GP (since $a_3=g_1$).
    Sum $g_2 + g_3 + g_4 = 4$.

    Block 1 sum ($a_1+a_2+a_3=2$).
    $a_3 = g_1$.

    $a_1, a_2$ free.
    Block 4 sum ($a_{10}, a_{11}, a_{12}=8$).
    $a_{10} = g_8 = g_1 q^7$.
    $a_{11}, a_{12}$ free.
    So valid.

    Another check:
    Could the GP be $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$ with $q$ such that terms are not aligned with block sums in a simple way?
    We used the fact that *any* 3 consecutive terms in GP sum to $S$, and next 3 sum to $q^3 S$.
    The block sums are sums of 3 consecutive terms.
    If a block is fully inside the GP, its sum is one of these "sums of 3 consecutive terms".
    We found that any length 9 interval contains at least 2 full blocks.
    Let these blocks be $B_i$ and $B_j$.
    Since they are full blocks, their indices are contiguous sets of 3 integers.
    Let $B_i$ be indices $\{x, x+1, x+2\}$.
    Let $B_j$ be indices $\{y, y+1, y+2\}$.
    Since they are disjoint blocks (partition of $\mathbb{N}$), and both inside $[k, k+8]$.
    And they are full blocks.
    Could they be non-consecutive?
    Say $B_i$ is block $n$, $B_j$ is block $n+2$.
    Then Block $n+1$ is between them.
    Indices of Block $n+1$ are between indices of Block $n$ and Block $n+2$.
    Since $[k, k+8]$ is an interval, it contains all integers between min index of $B_i$ and max index of $B_j$.
    So it must contain Block $n+1$ fully?
    Indices of $B_n$: $3n-2, 3n-1, 3n$.
    Indices of $B_{n+2}$: $3n+4, 3n+5, 3n+6$.
    Integers between: $3n+1, 3n+2, 3n+3$.
    These are exactly indices of Block $n+1$.
    So Block $n+1$ is fully contained.
    So yes, full blocks inside an interval must form a consecutive sequence of blocks.
    So we always have a pair of consecutive full blocks.
    Their sums ratio is $q^3$.
    Max ratio of consecutive block sums is at smallest indices.
    Blocks 1 and 2 ratio 2.
    But 1 and 2 cannot be the *only* full blocks (must include 3).
    Blocks 2 and 3 ratio 1.5.
    Can be the only full blocks.
    So max $q^3 = 1.5$.
    
    Is it possible to have a block sum ratio different from $q^3$?
    Only if the block sum doesn't correspond to a sum of 3 consecutive terms in GP?
    But a block *is* 3 consecutive terms in the sequence $a_n$.
    And since the block is inside the GP, these terms are consecutive in the GP too.
    So sum is exactly a sum of 3 consecutive terms in GP.
    So ratio must be $q^3$.
    
    Is it possible that the GP is $a_k, \dots, a_{k+8}$ but the terms in the block are not consecutive in the GP?
    No, a block is defined by indices $m, m+1, m+2$.
    These are consecutive integers.
    So they appear consecutively in any subsequence that includes them.
    
    So the logic holds firm.

    3.  This range must contain at least 2 complete blocks of 3 terms.

    5.  In a GP, the ratio of sum of 3 consecutive terms to the next 3 consecutive terms is $q^3$.
    6.  Thus, $\frac{2(n+1)}{2n} = q^3$ and $\frac{2(n+2)}{2(n+1)} = q^3$.
